Since then, it's been pretty continuous, and these feelings have been building and bothering me for about the last two months.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband’s diagnosis, have you had periods of feeling so down or anxious that you’ve lost interest in things, felt hopeless, or had thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I’ve been feeling extremely anxious and wound up, which has caused me to lose interest in and stop my usual activities like hiking, yoga, and knitting. While I do feel incredibly overwhelmed and guilty, I don't feel hopeless about the future, and I absolutely have no thoughts of hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been mainly tied to your husband’s diagnosis and the stress around it, or have you had similar problems before this in the absence of a major stressor?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before; these reactions are entirely new and directly tied to my husband's diagnosis and the sudden stress of managing his care. Before this, I was always highly organized, productive at work, and very active with my hobbies. I've never had these kinds of overwhelming difficulties functioning when there wasn't a major stressor.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to fall behind at work, have more conflict at home, or struggle with daily responsibilities more than you would expect from the stress alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely have. At work, my concentration has slipped so much that I've made avoidable errors on my inspection reports, which recently led to a tense performance review with my supervisor. I've also completely withdrawn from my hiking group and yoga classes, and I find myself snapping at coworkers because I feel too wound up to function normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any symptoms of a major depressive episode, such as persistent low mood most of the day, significant sleep or appetite changes, or feeling worthless or guilty nearly every day?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I definitely have trouble sleeping, and I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios instead of resting. I also feel a lot of guilt about trying to enjoy my hikes or yoga while my husband is dealing with his diagnosis. I haven't really had appetite changes or felt worthless, though.

Since then, it's been pretty continuous, and these feelings have been building and bothering me for about the last two months.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband’s diagnosis, have you had periods of feeling so down or anxious that you’ve lost interest in things, felt hopeless, or had thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I’ve been feeling extremely anxious and wound up, which has caused me to lose interest in and stop my usual activities like hiking, yoga, and knitting. While I do feel incredibly overwhelmed and guilty, I don't feel hopeless about the future, and I absolutely have no thoughts of hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been mainly tied to your husband’s diagnosis and the stress around it, or have you had similar problems before this in the absence of a major stressor?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before; these reactions are entirely new and directly tied to my husband's diagnosis and the sudden stress of managing his care. Before this, I was always highly organized, productive at work, and very active with my hobbies. I've never had these kinds of overwhelming difficulties functioning when there wasn't a major stressor.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to fall behind at work, have more conflict at home, or struggle with daily responsibilities more than you would expect from the stress alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely have. At work, my concentration has slipped so much that I've made avoidable errors on my inspection reports, which recently led to a tense performance review with my supervisor. I've also completely withdrawn from my hiking group and yoga classes, and I find myself snapping at coworkers because I feel too wound up to function normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any symptoms of a major depressive episode, such as persistent low mood most of the day, significant sleep or appetite changes, or feeling worthless or guilty nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I definitely have trouble sleeping, and I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios instead of resting. I also feel a lot of guilt about trying to enjoy my hikes or yoga while my husband is dealing with his diagnosis. I haven't really had appetite changes or felt worthless, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having panic attacks, or sudden episodes of intense fear with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest tightness, or feeling like you might lose control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any sudden panic attacks or physical symptoms like a racing heart or trouble breathing. It's more of a constant, heavy feeling of being wound up and overwhelmed rather than sudden bursts of intense fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares, flashbacks, or feeling constantly on edge or jumpy since your husband’s diagnosis?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any nightmares or flashbacks. But I do feel constantly wound up, keyed up, and completely unable to relax, which is why I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios.\n\nDoctor: Has the stressor been ongoing, and if it were to improve or end, do you think your symptoms would lessen within a few months?\n\nPatient: Yes, the stress has been completely ongoing since his diagnosis. I keep telling myself that once we get through this initial round of appointments and settle into a routine with his treatment, I'll be able to get back to my normal schedule and feel like myself again within a few months.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any period of at least 2 weeks since your husband’s diagnosis when you felt back to your usual self, or have the symptoms been present almost every day without a break?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't been any break at all; these feelings have been present almost every single day. Since these reactions started building about two months ago, I haven't had a single period where I felt back to my usual self. It has just been a continuous, daily struggle with feeling constantly wound up, irritable, and overwhelmed."

What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Ever since my husband’s diagnosis, I feel like I’m reacting to everything in a way I can’t manage, and it’s starting to interfere with how I function at work and at home. I'm just feeling incredibly overwhelmed and hoping to get some help on how to handle all of this.\n\nDoctor: How long after your husband’s diagnosis did these symptoms start, and have they been continuous since then?\n\nPatient: They started within just a few weeks of his diagnosis, right as we began coordinating his specialist appointments and treatment. Since then, it's been pretty continuous, and these feelings have been building and bothering me for about the last two months.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband’s diagnosis, have you had periods of feeling so down or anxious that you’ve lost interest in things, felt hopeless, or had thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I’ve been feeling extremely anxious and wound up, which has caused me to lose interest in and stop my usual activities like hiking, yoga, and knitting. While I do feel incredibly overwhelmed and guilty, I don't feel hopeless about the future, and I absolutely have no thoughts of hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been mainly tied to your husband’s diagnosis and the stress around it, or have you had similar problems before this in the absence of a major stressor?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before; these reactions are entirely new and directly tied to my husband's diagnosis and the sudden stress of managing his care. Before this, I was always highly organized, productive at work, and very active with my hobbies. I've never had these kinds of overwhelming difficulties functioning when there wasn't a major stressor.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to fall behind at work, have more conflict at home, or struggle with daily responsibilities more than you would expect from the stress alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely have. At work, my concentration has slipped so much that I've made avoidable errors on my inspection reports, which recently led to a tense performance review with my supervisor. I've also completely withdrawn from my hiking group and yoga classes, and I find myself snapping at coworkers because I feel too wound up to function normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any symptoms of a major depressive episode, such as persistent low mood most of the day, significant sleep or appetite changes, or feeling worthless or guilty nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I definitely have trouble sleeping, and I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios instead of resting. I also feel a lot of guilt about trying to enjoy my hikes or yoga while my husband is dealing with his diagnosis. I haven't really had appetite changes or felt worthless, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having panic attacks, or sudden episodes of intense fear with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest tightness, or feeling like you might lose control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any sudden panic attacks or physical symptoms like a racing heart or trouble breathing. It's more of a constant, heavy feeling of being wound up and overwhelmed rather than sudden bursts of intense fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares, flashbacks, or feeling constantly on edge or jumpy since your husband’s diagnosis?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any nightmares or flashbacks. But I do feel constantly wound up, keyed up, and completely unable to relax, which is why I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios.\n\nDoctor: Has the stressor been ongoing, and if it were to improve or end, do you think your symptoms would lessen within a few months?\n\nPatient: Yes, the stress has been completely ongoing since his diagnosis. I keep telling myself that once we get through this initial round of appointments and settle into a routine with his treatment, I'll be able to get back to my normal schedule and feel like myself again within a few months.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any period of at least 2 weeks since your husband’s diagnosis when you felt back to your usual self, or have the symptoms been present almost every day without a break?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't been any break at all; these feelings have been present almost every single day. Since these reactions started building about two months ago, I haven't had a single period where I felt back to my usual self. It has just been a continuous, daily struggle with feeling constantly wound up, irritable, and overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic event related to your husband’s diagnosis, such as feeling like he might die suddenly or that you were in immediate danger, with nightmares, flashbacks, or being constantly on edge because of that event?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. His Parkinson's diagnosis is incredibly stressful and overwhelming, but there was no immediate life-threatening scare or physical danger involved. Because of that, I haven't had any nightmares or flashbacks about it."

I fully expect that once we get through this initial wave of specialist appointments and settle into a treatment routine, I'll be able to get back to my normal schedule and feel like myself again."

I'm just feeling incredibly overwhelmed and hoping to get some help on how to handle all of this.\n\nDoctor: How long after your husband’s diagnosis did these symptoms start, and have they been continuous since then?\n\nPatient: They started within just a few weeks of his diagnosis, right as we began coordinating his specialist appointments and treatment. Since then, it's been pretty continuous, and these feelings have been building and bothering me for about the last two months.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband’s diagnosis, have you had periods of feeling so down or anxious that you’ve lost interest in things, felt hopeless, or had thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I’ve been feeling extremely anxious and wound up, which has caused me to lose interest in and stop my usual activities like hiking, yoga, and knitting. While I do feel incredibly overwhelmed and guilty, I don't feel hopeless about the future, and I absolutely have no thoughts of hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been mainly tied to your husband’s diagnosis and the stress around it, or have you had similar problems before this in the absence of a major stressor?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before; these reactions are entirely new and directly tied to my husband's diagnosis and the sudden stress of managing his care. Before this, I was always highly organized, productive at work, and very active with my hobbies. I've also completely withdrawn from my hiking group and yoga classes, and I find myself snapping at coworkers because I feel too wound up to function normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any symptoms of a major depressive episode, such as persistent low mood most of the day, significant sleep or appetite changes, or feeling worthless or guilty nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I definitely have trouble sleeping, and I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios instead of resting. But I do feel constantly wound up, keyed up, and completely unable to relax, which is why I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios.\n\nDoctor: Has the stressor been ongoing, and if it were to improve or end, do you think your symptoms would lessen within a few months?\n\nPatient: Yes, the stress has been completely ongoing since his diagnosis. Since these reactions started building about two months ago, I haven't had a single period where I felt back to my usual self. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Ever since my husband’s diagnosis, I feel like I’m reacting to everything in a way I can’t manage, and it’s starting to interfere with how I function at work and at home. I'm just feeling incredibly overwhelmed and hoping to get some help on how to handle all of this.\n\nDoctor: How long after your husband’s diagnosis did these symptoms start, and have they been continuous since then?\n\nPatient: They started within just a few weeks of his diagnosis, right as we began coordinating his specialist appointments and treatment. Since then, it's been pretty continuous, and these feelings have been building and bothering me for about the last two months.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband’s diagnosis, have you had periods of feeling so down or anxious that you’ve lost interest in things, felt hopeless, or had thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I’ve been feeling extremely anxious and wound up, which has caused me to lose interest in and stop my usual activities like hiking, yoga, and knitting. While I do feel incredibly overwhelmed and guilty, I don't feel hopeless about the future, and I absolutely have no thoughts of hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been mainly tied to your husband’s diagnosis and the stress around it, or have you had similar problems before this in the absence of a major stressor?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before; these reactions are entirely new and directly tied to my husband's diagnosis and the sudden stress of managing his care. Before this, I was always highly organized, productive at work, and very active with my hobbies. I've never had these kinds of overwhelming difficulties functioning when there wasn't a major stressor.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to fall behind at work, have more conflict at home, or struggle with daily responsibilities more than you would expect from the stress alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely have. At work, my concentration has slipped so much that I've made avoidable errors on my inspection reports, which recently led to a tense performance review with my supervisor. I've also completely withdrawn from my hiking group and yoga classes, and I find myself snapping at coworkers because I feel too wound up to function normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any symptoms of a major depressive episode, such as persistent low mood most of the day, significant sleep or appetite changes, or feeling worthless or guilty nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I definitely have trouble sleeping, and I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios instead of resting. I also feel a lot of guilt about trying to enjoy my hikes or yoga while my husband is dealing with his diagnosis. I haven't really had appetite changes or felt worthless, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having panic attacks, or sudden episodes of intense fear with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest tightness, or feeling like you might lose control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any sudden panic attacks or physical symptoms like a racing heart or trouble breathing. It's more of a constant, heavy feeling of being wound up and overwhelmed rather than sudden bursts of intense fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares, flashbacks, or feeling constantly on edge or jumpy since your husband’s diagnosis?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any nightmares or flashbacks. But I do feel constantly wound up, keyed up, and completely unable to relax, which is why I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios.\n\nDoctor: Has the stressor been ongoing, and if it were to improve or end, do you think your symptoms would lessen within a few months?\n\nPatient: Yes, the stress has been completely ongoing since his diagnosis. I keep telling myself that once we get through this initial round of appointments and settle into a routine with his treatment, I'll be able to get back to my normal schedule and feel like myself again within a few months.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any period of at least 2 weeks since your husband’s diagnosis when you felt back to your usual self, or have the symptoms been present almost every day without a break?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't been any break at all; these feelings have been present almost every single day. Since these reactions started building about two months ago, I haven't had a single period where I felt back to my usual self. It has just been a continuous, daily struggle with feeling constantly wound up, irritable, and overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic event related to your husband’s diagnosis, such as feeling like he might die suddenly or that you were in immediate danger, with nightmares, flashbacks, or being constantly on edge because of that event?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. His Parkinson's diagnosis is incredibly stressful and overwhelming, but there was no immediate life-threatening scare or physical danger involved. Because of that, I haven't had any nightmares or flashbacks about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this reaction lasted longer than 6 months, or do you expect it to improve once the stress around your husband’s diagnosis becomes more manageable?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't been six months yet—these symptoms have only been building for about the last two months. I fully expect that once we get through this initial wave of specialist appointments and settle into a treatment routine, I'll be able to get back to my normal schedule and feel like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed whether your symptoms are starting to ease at all as you get more used to the situation, or are they staying just as intense every day?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't eased up at all and have stayed quite intense over the past two months since the specialist visits began. I haven't felt any relief yet, but I keep telling myself that once we settle into a routine with his treatment, I'll be able to get back to my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling so overwhelmed that you’ve had trouble coping with daily responsibilities, or is the main issue more that you feel sad or depressed most of the day?"

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Ever since my husband’s diagnosis, I feel like I’m reacting to everything in a way I can’t manage, and it’s starting to interfere with how I function at work and at home. I'm just feeling incredibly overwhelmed and hoping to get some help on how to handle all of this.\n\nDoctor: How long after your husband’s diagnosis did these symptoms start, and have they been continuous since then?\n\nPatient: They started within just a few weeks of his diagnosis, right as we began coordinating his specialist appointments and treatment. Since then, it's been pretty continuous, and these feelings have been building and bothering me for about the last two months.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband’s diagnosis, have you had periods of feeling so down or anxious that you’ve lost interest in things, felt hopeless, or had thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I’ve been feeling extremely anxious and wound up, which has caused me to lose interest in and stop my usual activities like hiking, yoga, and knitting. While I do feel incredibly overwhelmed and guilty, I don't feel hopeless about the future, and I absolutely have no thoughts of hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been mainly tied to your husband’s diagnosis and the stress around it, or have you had similar problems before this in the absence of a major stressor?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before; these reactions are entirely new and directly tied to my husband's diagnosis and the sudden stress of managing his care. Before this, I was always highly organized, productive at work, and very active with my hobbies. I've never had these kinds of overwhelming difficulties functioning when there wasn't a major stressor.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to fall behind at work, have more conflict at home, or struggle with daily responsibilities more than you would expect from the stress alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely have. At work, my concentration has slipped so much that I've made avoidable errors on my inspection reports, which recently led to a tense performance review with my supervisor. I've also completely withdrawn from my hiking group and yoga classes, and I find myself snapping at coworkers because I feel too wound up to function normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any symptoms of a major depressive episode, such as persistent low mood most of the day, significant sleep or appetite changes, or feeling worthless or guilty nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I definitely have trouble sleeping, and I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios instead of resting. I also feel a lot of guilt about trying to enjoy my hikes or yoga while my husband is dealing with his diagnosis. I haven't really had appetite changes or felt worthless, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having panic attacks, or sudden episodes of intense fear with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest tightness, or feeling like you might lose control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any sudden panic attacks or physical symptoms like a racing heart or trouble breathing. It's more of a constant, heavy feeling of being wound up and overwhelmed rather than sudden bursts of intense fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares, flashbacks, or feeling constantly on edge or jumpy since your husband’s diagnosis?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any nightmares or flashbacks. But I do feel constantly wound up, keyed up, and completely unable to relax, which is why I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios.\n\nDoctor: Has the stressor been ongoing, and if it were to improve or end, do you think your symptoms would lessen within a few months?\n\nPatient: Yes, the stress has been completely ongoing since his diagnosis. I keep telling myself that once we get through this initial round of appointments and settle into a routine with his treatment, I'll be able to get back to my normal schedule and feel like myself again within a few months.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any period of at least 2 weeks since your husband’s diagnosis when you felt back to your usual self, or have the symptoms been present almost every day without a break?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't been any break at all; these feelings have been present almost every single day. Since these reactions started building about two months ago, I haven't had a single period where I felt back to my usual self. It has just been a continuous, daily struggle with feeling constantly wound up, irritable, and overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic event related to your husband’s diagnosis, such as feeling like he might die suddenly or that you were in immediate danger, with nightmares, flashbacks, or being constantly on edge because of that event?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. His Parkinson's diagnosis is incredibly stressful and overwhelming, but there was no immediate life-threatening scare or physical danger involved. Because of that, I haven't had any nightmares or flashbacks about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this reaction lasted longer than 6 months, or do you expect it to improve once the stress around your husband’s diagnosis becomes more manageable?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't been six months yet—these symptoms have only been building for about the last two months. I fully expect that once we get through this initial wave of specialist appointments and settle into a treatment routine, I'll be able to get back to my normal schedule and feel like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed whether your symptoms are starting to ease at all as you get more used to the situation, or are they staying just as intense every day?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't eased up at all and have stayed quite intense over the past two months since the specialist visits began. I haven't felt any relief yet, but I keep telling myself that once we settle into a routine with his treatment, I'll be able to get back to my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling so overwhelmed that you’ve had trouble coping with daily responsibilities, or is the main issue more that you feel sad or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: The main issue is definitely feeling completely overwhelmed and having trouble coping with my daily responsibilities. It has directly impacted my work, where my concentration has slipped so much that my supervisor noticed several errors on my inspection reports, and I've completely pulled away from my hiking group and yoga because I feel too wound up to manage them.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms begin within a few days of your husband’s diagnosis, or did they start later over the next few weeks? Also, were there any moments right after the diagnosis when you felt stunned, dazed, or unable to function at all?"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you to the clinic?\n\nPatient: Ever since my husband’s diagnosis, I feel like I’m reacting to everything in a way I can’t manage, and it’s starting to interfere with how I function at work and at home. I'm just feeling incredibly overwhelmed and hoping to get some help on how to handle all of this.\n\nDoctor: How long after your husband’s diagnosis did these symptoms start, and have they been continuous since then?\n\nPatient: They started within just a few weeks of his diagnosis, right as we began coordinating his specialist appointments and treatment. Since then, it's been pretty continuous, and these feelings have been building and bothering me for about the last two months.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband’s diagnosis, have you had periods of feeling so down or anxious that you’ve lost interest in things, felt hopeless, or had thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I’ve been feeling extremely anxious and wound up, which has caused me to lose interest in and stop my usual activities like hiking, yoga, and knitting. While I do feel incredibly overwhelmed and guilty, I don't feel hopeless about the future, and I absolutely have no thoughts of hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been mainly tied to your husband’s diagnosis and the stress around it, or have you had similar problems before this in the absence of a major stressor?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like this before; these reactions are entirely new and directly tied to my husband's diagnosis and the sudden stress of managing his care. Before this, I was always highly organized, productive at work, and very active with my hobbies. I've never had these kinds of overwhelming difficulties functioning when there wasn't a major stressor.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms caused you to fall behind at work, have more conflict at home, or struggle with daily responsibilities more than you would expect from the stress alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely have. At work, my concentration has slipped so much that I've made avoidable errors on my inspection reports, which recently led to a tense performance review with my supervisor. I've also completely withdrawn from my hiking group and yoga classes, and I find myself snapping at coworkers because I feel too wound up to function normally.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any symptoms of a major depressive episode, such as persistent low mood most of the day, significant sleep or appetite changes, or feeling worthless or guilty nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I definitely have trouble sleeping, and I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios instead of resting. I also feel a lot of guilt about trying to enjoy my hikes or yoga while my husband is dealing with his diagnosis. I haven't really had appetite changes or felt worthless, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having panic attacks, or sudden episodes of intense fear with symptoms like a racing heart, shortness of breath, chest tightness, or feeling like you might lose control?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any sudden panic attacks or physical symptoms like a racing heart or trouble breathing. It's more of a constant, heavy feeling of being wound up and overwhelmed rather than sudden bursts of intense fear.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares, flashbacks, or feeling constantly on edge or jumpy since your husband’s diagnosis?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any nightmares or flashbacks. But I do feel constantly wound up, keyed up, and completely unable to relax, which is why I lie awake at night running through worst-case scenarios.\n\nDoctor: Has the stressor been ongoing, and if it were to improve or end, do you think your symptoms would lessen within a few months?\n\nPatient: Yes, the stress has been completely ongoing since his diagnosis. I keep telling myself that once we get through this initial round of appointments and settle into a routine with his treatment, I'll be able to get back to my normal schedule and feel like myself again within a few months.\n\nDoctor: Has there been any period of at least 2 weeks since your husband’s diagnosis when you felt back to your usual self, or have the symptoms been present almost every day without a break?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't been any break at all; these feelings have been present almost every single day. Since these reactions started building about two months ago, I haven't had a single period where I felt back to my usual self. It has just been a continuous, daily struggle with feeling constantly wound up, irritable, and overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic event related to your husband’s diagnosis, such as feeling like he might die suddenly or that you were in immediate danger, with nightmares, flashbacks, or being constantly on edge because of that event?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. His Parkinson's diagnosis is incredibly stressful and overwhelming, but there was no immediate life-threatening scare or physical danger involved. Because of that, I haven't had any nightmares or flashbacks about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this reaction lasted longer than 6 months, or do you expect it to improve once the stress around your husband’s diagnosis becomes more manageable?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't been six months yet—these symptoms have only been building for about the last two months. I fully expect that once we get through this initial wave of specialist appointments and settle into a treatment routine, I'll be able to get back to my normal schedule and feel like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed whether your symptoms are starting to ease at all as you get more used to the situation, or are they staying just as intense every day?\n\nPatient: No, they haven't eased up at all and have stayed quite intense over the past two months since the specialist visits began. I haven't felt any relief yet, but I keep telling myself that once we settle into a routine with his treatment, I'll be able to get back to my normal self.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling so overwhelmed that you’ve had trouble coping with daily responsibilities, or is the main issue more that you feel sad or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: The main issue is definitely feeling completely overwhelmed and having trouble coping with my daily responsibilities. It has directly impacted my work, where my concentration has slipped so much that my supervisor noticed several errors on my inspection reports, and I've completely pulled away from my hiking group and yoga because I feel too wound up to manage them.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms begin within a few days of your husband’s diagnosis, or did they start later over the next few weeks? Also, were there any moments right after the diagnosis when you felt stunned, dazed, or unable to function at all?\n\nPatient: They didn't start right away within a few days, but rather developed over the next few weeks as I took on the burden of coordinating all his appointments and treatment. I actually didn't feel stunned or dazed at first; I immediately went into an active organizing mode to manage his care, and the overwhelmed feelings only built up later.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms continued for more than 6 months, or do you expect them to improve once the stress of your husband’s illness becomes more manageable?"

Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
